As AI models become ubiquitous in our daily lives, there has been an increasing demand for transparency in ML services. However, the model owner does not want to reveal the weights, as they are considered trade secrets. To solve this problem, researchers have turned to zero-knowledge proofs of ML model inference. These proofs convince the user that the ML model output is correct, without revealing the weights of the model to the user. Past work on these provers can be placed into two categories. The first method compiles the ML model into a low-level circuit, and proves the circuit using a ZK-SNARK. The second method uses custom cryptographic protocols designed only for a specific class of models. Unfortunately, the first method is highly inefficient, making it impractical for the large models used today, and the second method does not generalize well, making it difficult to update in the rapidly changing field of machine learning. To solve this, we propose ZKTorch , an open source end-to-end proving system that compiles ML models into base cryptographic operations called basic blocks, each proved using specialized protocols. ZKTorch is built on top of a novel parallel extension to the Mira accumulation scheme, enabling succinct proofs with minimal accumulation overhead. These contributions allow ZKTorch to achieve at least a 3 × 3\times 3 × reduction in the proof size compared to specialized protocols and up to a 6 × 6\times 6 × speedup in proving time over a general-purpose ZKML framework. Machine Learning and Zero Knowledge Proofs and Cryptographic Protocols 1. Introduction The widespread adoption of AI models, ranging from LLMs for text generation (Touvron et al . , 2023 ) to ResNet-50 (He et al . , 2016 ) for image classification, has driven demand for transparent and accountable AI (Bell, 2023 ) . However, there is a fundamental challenge of balancing transparency with protecting proprietary model weights. To address this, researchers have turned to zero-knowledge proofs (typically ZK-SNARKs) for ML model inference to perform trustless and weight privacy-preserving audits (Waiwitlikhit et al . , 2024 ; Kang et al . , 2022 ; Chen et al . , 2024 ; Lee et al . , 2020 ; Weng et al . , 2022 ; Feng et al . , 2021 ; Sun et al . , 2024 ; Liu et al . , 2021 ) . These proofs allow model providers to demonstrate that a model passed an audit without revealing its weights. ZK-SNARK-based approaches transform ML model inference into cryptographic operations in two general ways. One class of techniques encodes the model’s computation as a general arithmetic or logical circuit using a general-purpose ZK-SNARK system (Kang et al . , 2022 ) , such as halo2 (zcash contributors, 2022 ) . Another class designs bespoke cryptographic protocols for specific model types, such as convolutional neural networks (CNNs) or large language models (LLMs) (Lee et al . , 2020 ; Weng et al . , 2022 ; Sun et al . , 2024 ; Liu et al . , 2021 ) . Unfortunately, these methods either incur significant prover overhead or lack generality. General-purpose ZK-SNARK systems are not optimized for ML, leading to high overheads. Proving a single inference for a large model like Llama2-7B could take hundreds of hours, making this approach impractical. Bespoke cryptographic protocols, on the other hand, do not support a wide range of modern ML models. For instance, zkCNN does not support LLMs (Liu et al . , 2021 ) , and zkLLM does not support CNNs (Sun et al . , 2024 ) . To resolve this tension, we propose ZKTorch , the first end-to-end proof generation for every model in the MLPerf Edge Inference suite v4.1 (MLCommons, 2025 ) , surpassing the capabilities of prior ZKML systems. ZKTorch is an end-to-end system for compiling and proving ML model inference with both broad coverage and efficiency in mind. To support a wide variety of layers, ZKTorch adopts a modular design that compiles diverse ML models into a set of base cryptographic protocols designed to support common ML operations (referred to as basic blocks). However, this technique alone produces many small proofs for each basic block, resulting in large proof sizes. To address this issue, ZKTorch is built on top of the Mira accumulation scheme (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) , a pairing-based recursive SNARK designed for efficient composition of proofs. Mira offers two key advantages over prior work such as ProtoStar (Bünz and Chen, 2023 ) critical to ZKTorch : 1) it supports small recursive proofs for pairing-based arguments which enables scalable verification and succinct proofs and 2) it integrates naturally with efficient cryptographic primitives such as the KZG commitment scheme (Kate et al . , 2010 ) and state-of-the-art table lookup schemes (Eagen et al . , 2022 ) which are often used to prove nonlinearities in prior work (Chen et al . , 2024 ; Sun et al . , 2024 ) . These properties make Mira an ideal foundation for scalable ZKML systems, maintaining succinct proofs with minimal accumulation prover overhead. However, Mira only provides the accumulation protocol for three basic blocks in ZKTorch . We extend Mira to support pairing-based arguments for more basic blocks to further reap its benefits. Building on this foundation, ZKTorch provides a transpiler that maps high-level ML layers to basic blocks and a compiler that reorganizes models to optimize proving performance. As a result, ZKTorch supports 61 commonly used ML layers, including those found in CNNs, recurrent neural networks (RNNs), and transformers. To further scale proof generation, we generalize Mira’s accumulation protocol to support parallel aggregation. Mira performs accumulation by calculating a random linear combination between an accumulation instance and a SNARK proof. ZKTorch ’s accumulator changes a SNARK proof into an accumulation instance, allowing accumulation between two arbitrary accumulation instances. This approach alleviates the bottleneck of sequential recursion, significantly reducing overall proving time by up to 6 × 6\times 6 × . Moreover, the proof size of ZKTorch is at least 3 × 3\times 3 × smaller compared to specialized protocols such as Mystique (Weng et al . , 2021 ) and zkCNN (Liu et al . , 2021 ) . In summary, ZKTorch makes three key contributions: 1) it extends the set of base cryptographic protocols to support 61 commonly used ML layers across CNNs, RNNs, and LLMs; 2) it generalizes Mira’s accumulation protocol for parallel proof aggregation, achieving up to 6 × 6\times 6 × faster proving times and at least 3 × 3\times 3 × smaller proofs compared to specialized schemes; and 3) it incorporates a carefully designed compiler and transpiler that maximize the use of efficient basic blocks, resulting in up to a 4.8 × 4.8\times 4.8 × reduction in proving time and a 2 , 398 × 2,398\times 2 , 398 × reduction in proof size. Together, these contributions enable ZKTorch to combine flexibility and efficiency in a single framework, providing scalable zero-knowledge inference for real-world ML models. We open source ZKTorch and provide the link in Appendix A. 2. Preliminaries In this section, we provide the relevant background on important cryptographic primitives, ML models, and how they relate to ZK-SNARKs. We further discuss how specific considerations in ML models relate to the security of ZK-SNARKs for ML. Interactive Proofs and ZK-SNARKs An interactive proof Π = ( 𝒫 , 𝒱 ) Π 𝒫 𝒱 \Pi=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{V}) roman_Π = ( caligraphic_P , caligraphic_V ) for a relation ℛ ℛ \mathcal{R} caligraphic_R consists of an interactive protocol where the prover 𝒫 𝒫 \mathcal{P} caligraphic_P , who holds a witness w 𝑤 w italic_w , interacts with the verifier 𝒱 𝒱 \mathcal{V} caligraphic_V on public input 𝗉𝗂 𝗉𝗂 \mathsf{pi} sansserif_pi to generate a proof string π 𝜋 \pi italic_π to convince 𝒱 𝒱 \mathcal{V} caligraphic_V that ( 𝗉𝗂 , w ) ∈ ℛ 𝗉𝗂 𝑤 ℛ (\mathsf{pi},w)\in\mathcal{R} ( sansserif_pi , italic_w ) ∈ caligraphic_R . Building on top of interactive protocols, ZK-SNARKs are a class of cryptographic protocols that provide the following properties: 1) zero-knowledge, 2) non-interactivity, 3) succinctness, 4) knowledge soundness, and 5) completeness (Petkus, 2019 ) . Informally, the succinctness refers to the fact that the proof produced are small in size and can be verified quickly and the non-interactivity is achieved through Fiat-Shamir transform (Attema et al . , 2021 ) over a compatible interactive protocol. The security properties in this work are defined as follows: Perfect completeness. For every ( 𝗉𝗂 , w ) ∈ ℛ 𝗉𝗂 𝑤 ℛ (\mathsf{pi},w)\in\mathcal{R} ( sansserif_pi , italic_w ) ∈ caligraphic_R , Pr ⁡ [ 𝒱 ⁢ ( 𝗉𝗂 , 𝒫 ⁢ ( 𝗉𝗂 , w ) ) = accept ] = 1 Pr 𝒱 𝗉𝗂 𝒫 𝗉𝗂 𝑤 accept 1 \Pr[\mathcal{V}(\mathsf{pi},\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{pi},w))=\text{accept}]=1 roman_Pr [ caligraphic_V ( sansserif_pi , caligraphic_P ( sansserif_pi , italic_w ) ) = accept ] = 1 Knowledge soundness. For any probabilistic polynomial time (PPT) prover 𝒫 ∗ superscript 𝒫 \mathcal{P}^{*} caligraphic_P start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT , there exists a PPT extractor ℰ ℰ \mathcal{E} caligraphic_E such that given access to the entire executing process and the randomness of 𝒫 ∗ superscript 𝒫 \mathcal{P}^{*} caligraphic_P start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT , ℰ ℰ \mathcal{E} caligraphic_E can extract a witness w 𝑤 w italic_w such that π ∗ ← 𝒫 ∗ ⁢ ( 𝗉𝗂 , w ) ← superscript 𝜋 superscript 𝒫 𝗉𝗂 𝑤 \pi^{*}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}^{*}(\mathsf{pi},w) italic_π start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ← caligraphic_P start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ( sansserif_pi , italic_w ) , and w ← ℰ 𝒫 ∗ ⁢ ( 𝗉𝗂 , π ∗ ) ← 𝑤 superscript ℰ superscript 𝒫 𝗉𝗂 superscript 𝜋 w\leftarrow\mathcal{E}^{\mathcal{P}^{*}}(\mathsf{pi},\pi^{*}) italic_w ← caligraphic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT caligraphic_P start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ( sansserif_pi , italic_π start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) , the following inequality holds Pr ⁡ [ ( 𝗉𝗂 , w ) ∉ ℛ ∧ 𝒱 ⁢ ( 𝗉𝗂 , π ∗ ) = accept ] ≤ negl ⁢ ( λ ) Pr 𝗉𝗂 𝑤 ℛ 𝒱 𝗉𝗂 superscript 𝜋 accept negl 𝜆 \Pr[(\mathsf{pi},w)\not\in\mathcal{R}\land\mathcal{V}(\mathsf{pi},\pi^{*})=% \text{accept}]\leq\text{negl}(\lambda) roman_Pr [ ( sansserif_pi , italic_w ) ∉ caligraphic_R ∧ caligraphic_V ( sansserif_pi , italic_π start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) = accept ] ≤ negl ( italic_λ ) Zero knowledge. Let ⟨ 𝒫 , 𝒱 ∗ ⟩ 𝒫 superscript 𝒱 \langle\mathcal{P},\mathcal{V}^{*}\rangle ⟨ caligraphic_P , caligraphic_V start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ⟩ denote the set of all messages in the protocol (i.e., transcript). There exists a PPT simulator 𝒮 𝒮 \mathcal{S} caligraphic_S such that for any PPT algorithm 𝒱 ∗ superscript 𝒱 \mathcal{V}^{*} caligraphic_V start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT , ( 𝗉𝗂 , w ) ∈ ℛ 𝗉𝗂 𝑤 ℛ (\mathsf{pi},w)\in\mathcal{R} ( sansserif_pi , italic_w ) ∈ caligraphic_R , View ⁢ ( ⟨ 𝒫 , 𝒱 ∗ ⟩ ) ≈ 𝒮 𝒱 ∗ ⁢ ( 𝗉𝗂 ) View 𝒫 superscript 𝒱 superscript 𝒮 superscript 𝒱 𝗉𝗂 \text{View}\left(\langle\mathcal{P},\mathcal{V}^{*}\rangle\right)\approx% \mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{V}^{*}}(\mathsf{pi}) View ( ⟨ caligraphic_P , caligraphic_V start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ⟩ ) ≈ caligraphic_S start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT caligraphic_V start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ∗ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ( sansserif_pi ) holds. Note that the simulator 𝒮 𝒮 \mathcal{S} caligraphic_S has access to the trapdoor τ 𝜏 \tau italic_τ of the KZG commitment scheme, which allows the simulator to generate valid proofs without access to the witness w 𝑤 w italic_w . There are many ways to construct ZK-SNARKs, ranging from general-purpose circuits (zcash contributors, 2022 ) to specialized protocols for specific operations, such as ML models (Liu et al . , 2021 ) or identity protocols (Pauwels et al . , 2022 ) . Although protocols vary in their implementations, all ZK-SNARK protocols operate over finite fields and convert the computation to prove into an intermediate representation that is compiled into cryptographic operations. For example, the halo2 protocol uses a 256-bit field (several are admissible) and a randomized arithmetic intermediate representation (AIR) with preprocessing to represent computation (Gabizon, 2021 ) . Other proving systems use 64- or 32-bit fields, and other intermediate representations such as arithmetic circuits. Thus, there are two critical properties for ZK-SNARKs: the security of the underlying cryptographic protocol and the correctness of the intermediate representation. 2.3. Accumulation Schemes An accumulation scheme is an approach to fold multiple proofs into one, which reduces the proof size and the verifier’s overhead. We use the definition for an accumulation scheme from (Bünz and Chen, 2023 ) , repeated here. An accumulation scheme for a NARK (e.g., a basic block in ZKTorch ) ( 𝒫 𝖭𝖠𝖱𝖪 , 𝒱 𝖭𝖠𝖱𝖪 ) subscript 𝒫 𝖭𝖠𝖱𝖪 subscript 𝒱 𝖭𝖠𝖱𝖪 (\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{NARK}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathsf{NARK}}) ( caligraphic_P start_POSTSUBSCRIPT sansserif_NARK end_POSTSUBSCRIPT , caligraphic_V start_POSTSUBSCRIPT sansserif_NARK end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ) is a triple of algorithms acc = ( 𝒫 acc , 𝒱 acc , 𝒟 ) acc subscript 𝒫 acc subscript 𝒱 acc 𝒟 \text{acc}=(\mathcal{P}_{\text{acc}},\mathcal{V}_{\text{acc}},\mathcal{D}) acc = ( caligraphic_P start_POSTSUBSCRIPT acc end_POSTSUBSCRIPT , caligraphic_V start_POSTSUBSCRIPT acc end_POSTSUBSCRIPT , caligraphic_D ) , all of which have access to the same random oracle ρ acc subscript 𝜌 acc \rho_{\text{acc}} italic_ρ start_POSTSUBSCRIPT acc end_POSTSUBSCRIPT as well as ρ 𝖭𝖠𝖱𝖪 subscript 𝜌 𝖭𝖠𝖱𝖪 \rho_{\mathsf{NARK}} italic_ρ start_POSTSUBSCRIPT sansserif_NARK end_POSTSUBSCRIPT , the oracle for the NARK. The algorithms have the following syntax: • 𝒫 acc ( 𝗉𝗂 , π = ( π . x , π . w ) , acc = ( acc . x , acc . w ) ) → { acc ′ = ( acc ′ . x , acc ′ . w ) , 𝗉𝖿 } \mathcal{P}_{\text{acc}}(\mathsf{pi},\pi=(\pi.x,\pi.w),\text{acc}=(\text{acc}.% x,\text{acc}.w))\\ \rightarrow\{\text{acc}^{\prime}=(\text{acc}^{\prime}.x,\text{acc}^{\prime}.w)% ,\mathsf{pf}\} caligraphic_P start_POSTSUBSCRIPT acc end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ( sansserif_pi , italic_π = ( italic_π . italic_x , italic_π . italic_w ) , acc = ( acc . italic_x , acc . italic_w ) ) → { acc start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT = ( acc start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT . italic_x , acc start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT . italic_w ) , sansserif_pf } . The accumulation prover P acc subscript 𝑃 acc P_{\text{acc}} italic_P start_POSTSUBSCRIPT acc end_POSTSUBSCRIPT takes as input a statement 𝗉𝗂 𝗉𝗂 \mathsf{pi} sansserif_pi , NARK proof π 𝜋 \pi italic_π , and accumulator acc and outputs new accumulator acc ′ superscript acc ′ \text{acc}^{\prime} acc start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT and correction terms 𝗉𝖿 𝗉𝖿 \mathsf{pf} sansserif_pf . • 𝒱 acc ( 𝗉𝗂 , π . x , acc . x , acc ′ . x , 𝗉𝖿 ) → v \mathcal{V}_{\text{acc}}(\mathsf{pi},\pi.x,\text{acc}.x,\text{acc}^{\prime}.x,% \mathsf{pf})\rightarrow v caligraphic_V start_POSTSUBSCRIPT acc end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ( sansserif_pi , italic_π . italic_x , acc . italic_x , acc start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT . italic_x , sansserif_pf ) → italic_v . The accumulation verifier takes as input the statement 𝗉𝗂 𝗉𝗂 \mathsf{pi} sansserif_pi , the instances of the NARK proof, the old and new accumulator, the correction terms, and accepts by outputting 0 and rejects otherwise. • 𝒟 ⁢ ( acc ) → v → 𝒟 acc 𝑣 \mathcal{D}(\text{acc})\rightarrow v caligraphic_D ( acc ) → italic_v . The decider on input acc accepts by outputting 0 and rejects otherwise. Note that x 𝑥 x italic_x and w 𝑤 w italic_w in the above definition can be understood as the public and private parts of the instances. Mira (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) enables a pairing-based SNARK prover to perform accumulation on elliptic group elements (i.e., the proofs and the commitments of witnesses) without needing to perform accumulation on both plaintext field elements and their commitments as required in previous protocols such as ProtoStar (Bünz and Chen, 2023 ) . Specifically, Mira performs accumulation by calculating a random linear combination between an accumulation instance and a SNARK proof (and the corresponding input/output commitments used in the pairing check of the SNARK). Mira also shows how to derive the error when performing the elliptic pairing check on the accumulation instance. We apply Mira to fold multiple proofs from CQ and CQLin into one. Since there are other basic blocks in ZKTorch also based on low-degree algebraic tests, we extend the accumulation scheme for them. We provide a high-level introduction to the Mira accumulation scheme (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) here. 𝒟 𝒟 \mathcal{D} caligraphic_D accepts the acc if the relaxed algebraic test 𝖵 lin subscript 𝖵 lin \mathsf{V}_{\text{lin}} sansserif_V start_POSTSUBSCRIPT lin end_POSTSUBSCRIPT holds. The checks for commitment openings can be omitted because Mira uses trivial identity commitment (i.e., 𝖢𝗈𝗆𝗆𝗂𝗍 𝖢𝗈𝗆𝗆𝗂𝗍 \mathsf{Commit} sansserif_Commit returns the input itself, which means [ C i ] = [ m i ] , ∀ i delimited-[] subscript 𝐶 𝑖 delimited-[] subscript 𝑚 𝑖 for-all 𝑖 [C_{i}]=[m_{i}],\;\forall i [ italic_C start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ] = [ italic_m start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ] , ∀ italic_i and E = e 𝐸 𝑒 E=e italic_E = italic_e ). Relying on the homomorphism of the underlying commitment scheme, the accumulation prover can efficiently fold proofs. However, this formulation constrains us to perform accumulation sequentially. Given that there are N 𝑁 N italic_N proofs to fold, the time complexity will be O ⁢ ( N ) 𝑂 𝑁 O(N) italic_O ( italic_N ) . To improve this time complexity, we view π 𝜋 \pi italic_π as a special case of acc and generalize the above definition in Sec 4.4 to enable parallel accumulation. 2.4. ML Models and ZK-SNARKs ML models consist of highly structured computation that are typically described as layers that take as input one or more tensors . These layers include operations ranging from matrix multiplication to the ReLU non-linearity (LeCun et al . , 2015 ) . Some of the tensors are fixed ahead of time (i.e., are weights ) and others must be computed dynamically depending on the input. (LeCun et al . , 2015 ) provides a detailed description of ML model computation for further reading. ML model computation is natively computed in floating-point arithmetic, which is not natively representable in finite fields. To address this issue, ZK-SNARK systems for ML approximate the floating-point values with fixed-point arithmetic, where a number is represented by a value and a scale factor (Chen et al . , 2024 ) . ML models vary in their computational patterns, but within a model, computational patterns are often repeated. For example, the widely used ResNet series alternates between convolutions, residual layers, and ReLU (He et al . , 2016 ) . LLMs use feed-forward networks (matrix multiplications) and attention heads (matrix multiplication, point-wise multiplication, non-linearities, and the softmax) (Touvron et al . , 2023 ) . A key insight we leverage to construct ZKTorch is that the number of unique operations used by common ML models is relatively small. Consider the widely used MLPerf inference benchmark (Reddi et al . , 2020 ) , which covers a wide range of ML models. Across all of the models in the edge category, only 61 operations are used. These operations further cover other popular models, such as the Llama LLM series. Lookup Arguments. Lookup arguments are NARKs for proving set inclusion. Given a table T 𝑇 T italic_T known to both the prover and verifier, the prover can prove that all elements of a committed vector are in T 𝑇 T italic_T . Table sizes to support scaling and non-linearities are often much larger than the committed vector size, so we use the cq protocol (Eagen et al . , 2022 ) because it is compatible with the KZG commitment scheme and has proving time independent of the table size. 3. Overview of ZKTorch Figure 1. Architecture diagram of ZKTorch ZKTorch is designed to bridge the gap between broad coverage of ML layers and efficient proof generation, based on the key insights and contributions described in Section 1 . In particular, ZKTorch extends pairing-based cryptographic techniques to support 61 common layers across CNNs, RNNs, and transformers, and generalizes Mira’s accumulation protocol to enable parallel proof aggregation with minimal overhead. With a carefully optimized compiler and transpiler, ZKTorch provides an end-to-end framework for scalable zero-knowledge proofs of real-world ML model inference. At a high level, ZKTorch compiles an ONNX model graph, consisting of various ML layers and operations, into a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of basic blocks , which are low-level zero-knowledge primitives tailored to efficiently handle common ML operations while preserving weight or input privacy. We illustrate this architecture in Figure 1 . ZKTorch leverages • an optimizing compiler to reorder and combine model layers to prove them with less overhead, • a transpiler to automatically translate ML layers in the ONNX graph into compositions of these basic blocks, and • a set of cryptographic primitives based on KZG polynomial commitments (Kate et al . , 2010 ) implemented using arkworks libraries (arkworks contributors, 2022 ) to support a diverse set of ML layers and parallel proof aggregation by extending Mira (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) . Witness generation and encoding. To prove correctness of inference without revealing internal weights or inputs, ZKTorch adopts a witness-generation process reminiscent of traditional model inference. This process scales the relevant tensors to elements of a finite prime field and encodes them by interpreting their final dimension as polynomials. The prover commits to them using KZG for each tensor (input, output, and intermediate activations). Layer-by-layer proving. With these commitments, ZKTorch proves layer-by-layer that each basic block within the layer was computed correctly. Each basic block’s output commitments become inputs to subsequent blocks in the DAG, and ZKTorch maintains intermediate proofs to ensure the correctness of every step. Parallel proof accumulation. Naively proving and verifying every basic block in sequence would lead to large proof sizes and high overall overhead. To address this, ZKTorch supports parallel accumulation. After generating proofs for the basic blocks, ZKTorch aggregates them—often batching proofs for the same block type—into a single succinct proof. This generalization of Mira’s accumulation protocol significantly reduces total proving time (by up to 6 × 6\times 6 × in our measurements) while maintaining lightweight proof sizes (at least 3 × 3\times 3 × smaller than specialized protocols). Scalability and full coverage. Finally, ZKTorch demonstrates end-to-end proofs of entire models on a single server, rather than focusing on just one or two specialized network topologies. Thanks to its comprehensive set of basic blocks and optimized compilation pipeline, ZKTorch can handle all models in the MLPerf Inference: Edge v4.1 suite (MLCommons, 2025 ) , including CNNs, RNNs, and transformer-based architectures. In doing so, ZKTorch illustrates for the first time how a general-purpose ZKML system can combine flexibility and efficiency for modern, real-world ML models. 4. Basic Blocks Table 1. List of basic blocks rules implemented in ZKTorch . ✓indicates that the accumulation scheme is supported. Basic Block Category Accumulation Add Arithmetic Operations ✓ Div Arithmetic Operations Mod Arithmetic Operations Mul Arithmetic Operations ✓ MulConst Arithmetic Operations ✓ MulScalar Arithmetic Operations ✓ Sub Arithmetic Operations ✓ Sum Arithmetic Operations ✓ BooleanCheck Boolean Operations Eq Boolean Operations ✓ MaxProof Max Concat Shape Operations ✓ CopyConstraint Shape Operations Permute Shape Operations ✓ CQ Tables ✓ CQ2 Tables ✓ CQLin Matrix Multiplication ✓ MatMul Matrix Multiplication ✓ OneToOne Sorting Checks Ordered Sorting Checks The first major component of ZKTorch are basic blocks. They are selected to prove common operations in ML model layers. We show a list of basic blocks in Table 1 along with a categorization of the kinds of basic blocks. These include arithmetic operations (e.g., vector addition), matrix multiplication, tables (e.g., pointwise non-linearities), and shape operations (e.g., permutations). Among all the basic blocks, we can support parallel accumulation for 13 basic blocks. In our compiler and transpiler, we optimize our layer implementations to avoid using the remaining seven basic blocks, which only appear in 18 infrequent layers out of the 61 total layers. In this section, we first provide the high-level description for each category of basic block. We then argue that the security properties are retained through composition. To effectively fold proofs from several basic blocks into a lightweight final proof, we show the generalized accumulation scheme and its security properties. For each basic block supporting accumulation, we introduce how we relax the algebraic test to make the proof foldable. For other basic blocks, we show the techniques to prove these special operations. 4.1. Basic Block Types Arithmetic and Boolean Operations. These are used in arithmetic and Boolean layers and for intermediate computations in computational layers. Max. This basic block is used for Max, Min, and MaxPool layers. Shape Operations. The concat, copy constraint, and permute basic blocks prove shape operations. We perform shape operations in shape layers such as Concat and Transpose, as well as in computational layers that require inputs to be transformed before applying a basic block. For example, we may need to transpose an input to the MatMul layer before performing a matrix multiplication. The concat basic block is used for concatenation, while the permute basic block is used for many reshape or transpose operations. In special cases, we use the copy-constraint basic block to prove that every element in the output comes from a value in the input. Tables. We implement the CQ and CQ2 basic blocks based on the CQ protocol (Eagen et al . , 2022 ) . CQ checks for set inclusion, while CQ2 checks for lookup table inclusion. The set and lookup table must be known at compile time. A common use case of CQ includes checking that all values of a committed vector are nonnegative. For example, to prove the Div layer, we must check that the resulting remainders after division are nonnegative. CQ2 is used to prove nonlinearities and scale factor correction after multiplication and division operations. Matrix Multiplication. We use the CQLin and MatMul basic blocks for proving matrix multiplication. CQLin implements the CQLin protocol (Eagen and Gabizon, 2023 ) which optimizes matrix multiplication when one matrix is fixed, such as in convolutions in CNNs. Since the matrix is known at compile time, it can be processed during setup to enable a proving time linear in the input matrix size. MatMul proves matrix multiplication generally and is based on the Aurora protocol (Ben-Sasson et al . , 2018 ) , and is used for many LLM and RNN matrix multiplications. Sorting Checks. We support the OneToOne and Ordered basic blocks to check that there is a one-to-one mapping between the input and output and that the input is sorted, respectively. These are currently only used for the ArgMax and TopK layers. 4.2. Parallel Accumulation We provide a formal description of the accumulation scheme, generalizing the method in Sec. 3.3 of (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) to support parallel accumulation in ZKTorch . We build upon the Mira accumulation scheme, which only supports sequential accumulation. Specifically, we replace the π 𝜋 \pi italic_π in base Mira with an acc instance, and show that π 𝜋 \pi italic_π is a special case of acc . This method allows ZKTorch to perform accumulation between two arbitrary acc instances instead of requiring one of them to be π 𝜋 \pi italic_π , allowing for parallel accumulation with techniques such as Merkle tree reduction (i.e. aggregate instances at each level of the tree in parallel). We use the notation described in Sec. 2.3 to describe the generalized Mira protocol. In our formulation, the NARK proof π 𝜋 \pi italic_π is simply a special case of the accumulator, in which μ = 1 𝜇 1 \mu=1 italic_μ = 1 and e = 0 ℓ 𝑒 superscript 0 ℓ e=0^{\ell} italic_e = 0 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT . Completeness. If the prover 𝒫 𝒫 \mathcal{P} caligraphic_P has two instances of accumulators that can pass the verifier and decider checks, the aggregated instance will also be accepted with probability 1 1 1 1 following the homomorphism of the commitment scheme. Knowledge soundness. We provide the proof of knowledge soundness in Appendix C. The high-level idea is that we replace the algebraic tests in our generalized Mira with the algebraic checks defined in (Bünz and Chen, 2023 ) , which allows us to use similar techniques to prove that the protocol is knowledge sound. Then we use the techniques in (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) to argue that our protocol retains knowledge soundness. Zero knowledge. Given that the transcript between the simulator and the adversary and that between the prover and the adversary are indistinguishable, the accumulation scheme satisfies zero knowledge. 4.3. Security of Composing Basic Blocks In the remainder of this section, we first show that composing basic blocks satisfying certain properties remains secure. We then prove the security of our individual basic blocks. We represent the computation of a model inference as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), where vertices are basic blocks and edges denote where the inputs flow. We use the ZKTorch compiler and transpiler to transform the original ONNX graph into a ZKTorch DAG that consists of basic blocks. All our protocols are complete and knowledge sound. If any node in the DAG is invalid but accepted, the entire DAG is invalid. Let the probability of node v i subscript 𝑣 𝑖 v_{i} italic_v start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT being unsound be e v i subscript 𝑒 subscript 𝑣 𝑖 e_{v_{i}} italic_e start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_v start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT . Then, the probability of the DAG being invalid is at most ∑ e v i subscript 𝑒 subscript 𝑣 𝑖 \sum e_{v_{i}} ∑ italic_e start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_v start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT by the union bound. Note that in practice, we have at most 100,000 nodes in our DAG, so we can achieve a total soundness error of e 𝑒 e italic_e by setting the threshold of each individual node at 1 10 6 ⁢ e 1 superscript 10 6 𝑒 \frac{1}{10^{6}}e divide start_ARG 1 end_ARG start_ARG 10 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 6 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_ARG italic_e . In practice, due to our field size, this error is negligible. Our definition of zero knowledge is equivalent to the black-box simulator zero knowledge defined in (Goldreich and Oren, 1994 ) . Since our protocol satisfies this definition, it is also auxiliary-input zero knowledge, and therefore has the composability property defined in (Goldreich and Oren, 1994 ) which shows the full DAG is zero knowledge as well. 4.4. Basic Block Protocols We first discuss the relaxation of the algebraic tests for the basic blocks supported in the parallel accumulation protocol. Then we discuss a polynomial interactive oracle proof (Poly-IOP) that the other basic blocks are based on and demonstrate the techniques to prove these special operations. 4.4.1. Foldable basic blocks We support parallel accumulation for 13 basic blocks. Here we introduce the relaxed algebraic test for each basic block. We skip protocols based on cached quotients and refer the readers to Mira (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) . Sum, MatMul, and Permute. We prove these protocols based on the techniques in Aurora (Ben-Sasson et al . , 2018 ) . Basic blocks based on a Poly-IOP and Div/Mod The following basic blocks are not accumulated because their proofs contain grand product arguments, creating high-degree constraints, or in the case of Div and Mod they batch the checks for individual division and modulus operations across the tensor into a single check. We prove Div and Mod with the techniques in (Chen et al . , 2024 ) . For the other basic blocks, we show how to prove them with a poly-IOP. The Poly-IOP has perfect completeness, knowledge soundness, and zero knowledge. The proofs are provided in Appendix B. Custom protocols. We describe four ZKTorch protocols that can be built on top of Poly-IOP in this subsubsection. (1) BooleanCheck : The goal of BooleanCheck is to ensure f ⁢ ( x ) 𝑓 𝑥 f(x) italic_f ( italic_x ) are either 0 0 or 1 1 1 1 for x = ω i , ∀ i ∈ [ n ] formulae-sequence 𝑥 superscript 𝜔 𝑖 for-all 𝑖 delimited-[] 𝑛 x=\omega^{i},\forall i\in[n] italic_x = italic_ω start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT , ∀ italic_i ∈ [ italic_n ] . (2) OrderedCheck : Given two degree- n 𝑛 n italic_n polynomials f ⁢ ( x ) 𝑓 𝑥 f(x) italic_f ( italic_x ) and g ⁢ ( x ) 𝑔 𝑥 g(x) italic_g ( italic_x ) , we want to prove that the sequence g ⁢ ( ω 0 ) , … , g ⁢ ( ω n − 1 ) 𝑔 superscript 𝜔 0 … 𝑔 superscript 𝜔 𝑛 1 g(\omega^{0}),\dots,\allowbreak g(\omega^{n-1}) italic_g ( italic_ω start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 0 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) , … , italic_g ( italic_ω start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_n - 1 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) is sorted in descending or ascending order with respect to the sequence f ⁢ ( ω 0 ) , … , f ⁢ ( ω n − 1 ) 𝑓 superscript 𝜔 0 … 𝑓 superscript 𝜔 𝑛 1 f(\omega^{0}),\dots,f(\omega^{n-1}) italic_f ( italic_ω start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 0 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) , … , italic_f ( italic_ω start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_n - 1 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) . end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ( italic_X ) end_CELL end_ROW 5. Compiler and Transpiler The remaining key components in ZKTorch are the compiler and transpiler. With these components, ZKTorch can transform a given ML model into the underlying basic blocks. Since the prover efficiency of different basic blocks varies and ZKTorch does not support accumulation for some special basic blocks, we need to carefully design the transformation rules to keep ZKTorch performant in terms of the prover overhead and the proof size. There are two major challenges in constructing such a compiler. First, we must optimize within each layer for performance while maintaining high accuracy. For example, when performing shape operations, we opt for the cheapest sequence of operations. For some basic blocks, we choose to prioritize accuracy, such as softmax. The softmax function over a vector of size K 𝐾 K italic_K elementwise is σ ⁢ ( 𝐳 ) i = e z i / ∑ j = 1 K e z j 𝜎 subscript 𝐳 𝑖 superscript 𝑒 subscript 𝑧 𝑖 superscript subscript 𝑗 1 𝐾 superscript 𝑒 subscript 𝑧 𝑗 \sigma(\mathbf{z})_{i}=e^{z_{i}}/\sum_{j=1}^{K}e^{z_{j}} italic_σ ( bold_z ) start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT = italic_e start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT / ∑ start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_j = 1 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_K end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_e start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_j end_POSTSUBSCRIPT end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT . This can naively be implemented with an exponentiation, sum, and division. However, the elements of the input 𝐳 𝐳 \mathbf{z} bold_z can result in exponentiation exceeding the lookup table range. Instead, we compute m = max ⁡ ( 𝐳 ) 𝑚 𝐳 m=\max(\mathbf{z}) italic_m = roman_max ( bold_z ) , and the exponentiation e z i − m superscript 𝑒 subscript 𝑧 𝑖 𝑚 e^{z_{i}-m} italic_e start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT - italic_m end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT which will be a small value since the exponent is negative. Second, we must optimize across layers for high performance. For this, we create graph transformation rules that prune the DAG of basic blocks and combine basic blocks as well. We support all the MLPerf Inference: Edge V4.1 models (MLCommons, 2025 ) , which include state-of-the-art LLMs, CNNs, and RNNs. We now provide an overview of ZKTorch ’s compiler before discussing its implementation. 5.1. Overview ZKTorch consists of three steps: 1) graph compilation to optimize ML layers for cryptographic proving as opposed to standard inference, 2) transpilation of the compiled ML models to a DAG composed of basic blocks, and 3) proving and accumulation on the resulting DAG. Speicifically, ZKTorch ’s compiler takes as input an ML model specification (we use ONNX for our implementation) and outputs an optimized, compiled ML model (another optimized ONNX) that will be used in the downstream task. The transpiler then takes the optimized ML model specification and outputs a program specific to the model at hand. Furthermore, several parts of the proving process can be precomputed per-model to reduce the overall proving time per inference. In our current implementation, we use rule-based graph transformations for the model compilation and transpilation. We now describe the different transformation rules in ZKTorch compiler. 5.2. Graph Transformation Rules Graph transformations (compilation) are a widely used method to improve ML model performance. The same operation can be computed in many different ways, which have different performance characteristics on different hardware platforms for standard ML inference. Similarly, the performance characteristics of identical methods of computation widely vary for our basic blocks. Our graph transformation implementation takes as input an ONNX file and outputs an ONNX file optimized for ZKTorch . We use rule-based transformations to use fewer or cheaper cryptographic operations. In this work, we implement seven transformation rules and apply them greedily until the graph does not change. This optimization method is commonly used in compilers (Alfred et al . , 2007 ) . Table 2. List of graph transformation rules we currently implement in ZKTorch . Figure 2 a shows GeLU. Rule name Model Reason Description ReshapeTrans Bert Decreases number of permutes from 2 to 1 Replace every Reshape node followed by a Transpose node with a custom ReshapeTrans node. GeLU GPTj Decreases number of CQs from 6 to 1 Replace every subgraph that represents GeLU with one GeLU node. MultiHeadMatMul GPTj Uses no copy constraint Replace every subgraph that represents MultiHead MatMul with one custom MultiHeadMatMul node. RoPE GPTj Uses no copy constraint Replace every subgraph that represents RoPE operation with two custom nodes (RoPEConst and RoPERotate). CustomCNN ResNet, 3DUnet, RetinaNet Uses no copy constraint, cheap aggregation Execute convolutions by viewing intermediate tensors as channels second. This allows us to use only cqlin and add. MultiHeadConv RetinaNet Uses no copy constraint Replace conv followed by reshape and transpose with a custom MultiHeadConv. ConcatConv 3DUnet Uses no copy constraint Replace two conv followed by a concat with a custom ConcatConv to avoid the concat. Figure 2. a) One CQ for GeLU, b) CQLin and CQ for MatMul with one fixed matrix We provide a summary of the rules we use in Table 2 . Our graph transformation rules do not change the overall model architecture. They instead replace common layer patterns in ONNX files with an optimized implementation. For example, the GeLU operation in an ONNX file is expressed as 8 different arithmetic layers including Pow and Tanh. We can replace these layers with a single GeLU layer that is implemented with a single table lookup that directly performs the GeLU mapping. This transformation is shown in Figure 2 . Our transformation rules can provide up to a 10 × 10\times 10 × speedup for the operations at hand and up to a 4.8 × 4.8\times 4.8 × speedup for real-world models. Moreover, ZKTorch ’s graph transformation rules are easily extensible and can be updated to accommodate newer models. 5.3. Transpilation Given an optimized graph, ZKTorch ’s final step is to transpile the graph into an executable cryptographic protocol. Similar to our graph transformation rules, we currently implement our transpiler with rule-based transformations. We implement 61 layers in the transpiler, corresponding to ONNX layers. Due to space limitations, we cannot fully describe all of the layer implementations here. Instead, we describe classes of layers and highlight specific instances for illustrative purposes. Before describing the classes of layers, we highlight several common patterns. First, any layer that multiplies two numbers must have a rescaling at some point after that layer to correct for the scale factor (Chen et al . , 2024 ) . Second, due to the way we lay out the committed intermediate values, which we call activations , certain operations can be free. Our graph transformations aim to lay out the activations so that they use as few expensive shape operations as possible. MatMul-based layers. The Gemm, MatMul, LSTM, Conv, and ConvTranspose layers are implemented with matrix multiplication operations. When one matrix is known at compile time, such as the weights in Conv or with a fixed MatMul matrix, we prove matrix multiplication using CQLin. Figure 2 b shows how a MatMul layer is transformed into basic blocks when one matrix is fixed. Shape operations. ML models frequently employ layer operations that manipulate array shapes, including Reshape, Concat, Split, and Transpose operations. For both input and output tensors, polynomial commitments are made along the tensor’s final axis. When shape operations preserve this final axis, no new polynomial commitments are required for the outputs. However, many such operations still modify the final axis. In this case, transpose and most reshape operations can be proven using our Aurora-based Permute protocol. For more specialized cases involving element copying, we implement CopyConstraint, which offers the most flexibility but comes with higher computational costs. For example, we must still use CopyConstraint in the Slice layer, where we want to copy a subset of consecutive elements into a new vector. Pointwise non-linearities. We use the CQ2 basic block to verify nonlinearities and scale factor corrections after multiplication and division operations. Whenever possible, we combine nonlinearities and scaling operations within a layer to reduce the number of required lookup proofs. Since CQ2 is accumulable, the number of nonlinearities in an ML model does not affect performance, which means that replacing any nonlinearity with another in ZKTorch does not increase the prover overhead or proof size. Arithmetic operations. Most arithmetic layers such as Add, Mul are trivially implemented using a corresponding basic block. We describe two examples using custom basic blocks: And : To implement And, we use BooleanCheck to check that each input array only contains 0s or 1s, and then use Mul to obtain their product. ArgMax : To implement ArgMax, we use OrderedCheck to check that the sorted input is in descending order, One-to-oneCheck to check that the sorted elements correspond to the original unsorted input, and CopyConstraint to select the first element. Bespoke operations. To efficiently prove ML models, ZKTorch replaces some specific patterns of ONNX nodes with customized operations. This is because if ZKTorch directly encodes and proves based on the original tensors, the proving overhead will be too expensive for practical usage. Here we describe two examples of bespoke operations: CustomConv : We can replace all the Conv with CustomConv in CNNs, achieving the same accuracy as Conv, while saving memory, proving time, and proof size by removing the need for any shape operation in Convolution. For example, in a 2D convolution, rather than feeding in an input of size [ B , I , H , W ] 𝐵 𝐼 𝐻 𝑊 [B,I,H,W] [ italic_B , italic_I , italic_H , italic_W ] , ZKTorch makes all input sizes become [ B ⁢ H ⁢ W , I ] 𝐵 𝐻 𝑊 𝐼 [BHW,I] [ italic_B italic_H italic_W , italic_I ] . For example, suppose we want to perform a 2D convolution on an image with a tensor of shape C × D × D 𝐶 𝐷 𝐷 C\times D\times D italic_C × italic_D × italic_D . We first need 32 × 32 32 32 32\times 32 32 × 32 CQLins to change the number of channels from 3 3 3 3 to C 𝐶 C italic_C . We perform the almost-free additions introduced in Section 5.3 to generate the D × D 𝐷 𝐷 D\times D italic_D × italic_D tensor. We finally need D × D 𝐷 𝐷 D\times D italic_D × italic_D CQs to scale the output back. We can fold the proofs into only one CQLin, one CQ, and one addition in the end with generalized Mira. To prevent using multiple CQ and Mul basic blocks, we combine all operations into one CQ. 6. Experiments We now turn to evaluating ZKTorch . We show that ZKTorch can prove inference on a wider range of model than prior work: it can prove all of the MLPerf edge inference models on a single server. 6.1. Experimental Setup Table 3. List of models considered in the evaluation with their parameters and input dimensions. Model Part Parameters Input dimensions BERT 110M 1 token GPT-j 6B 2 tokens LLaMA-2-7B 7B 1 token RNNT 120M seqlen=1, batch=1 SDXL Text encoder 3.5B 1 token Text encoder 2 1 token VAE decoder 1x4x4x4 UNet 1x4x4x4, seqlen=1 ResNet-50 25.6M 1x3x224x224 RetinaNet 34M 1x3x800x800 3D-UNet 19M 1x1x128x128x128 We evaluate proving time, verification time, proof size, and accuracy on all models in the MLPerf Inference v4.1 Edge benchmark, as well as RNNT from v4.0 and LLaMA-2-7B. These include ResNet50-v1.5, RetinaNet 800x800, BERT, 3D-Unet, GPT-j, and Stable-Diffusion-XL. The number of parameters and input sizes we used for each model is shown in Table 3 . Our experiments are all conducted on a pairing-friendly elliptic curve BN254 over a 254-bit prime field. For all models we use 10 bits of quantization except for GPT-j, where we used 12 bits. Proving and verification performance are measured on a single server with an Intel(R) Xeon(R) Platinum 8358 2.6 GHz processor, with 64 threads and 4 TB memory. 6.2. Comparisons with Prior Work We compare the performance of ZKTorch to the general-purpose framework ZKML (Chen et al . , 2024 ) by benchmarking the most expensive model GPT-2 in ZKML. ZKTorch achieves a proving time of 599s, representing a 6 × \times × speedup (ZKML proves GPT-2 in 3601s within the identical hardware environment). Furthermore, the verification time is reduced to 12s, outperforming the 19s reported by ZKML. Another general proving is ezkl (Zkonduit Inc., 2023 ) , which we do not compare against since zkml is faster than ezkl due to its circuit optimizer. Concretely, ezkl on nanoGPT small (1-million parameters, 26M flops) needs 966s to prove, while zkml on a larger LLM GPT-2 (117-million parameters, 189M flops) requires only 159s to prove per token. Considering this, we only used zkml in the evaluation to compare the prover performance. When comparing proof sizes, we find that ZKTorch even outperforms specialized protocols including zkCNN (Liu et al . , 2021 ) , Mystique (Weng et al . , 2021 ) , and pvCNN (Weng et al . , 2022 ) by at least 3 × 3\times 3 × due to the adoption of the accumulation scheme, as shown in Table 6 . Following Mira (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) , we do not compare the performance of zkLLM (Sun et al . , 2024 ) with ZKTorch for the same reason: zkLLM is GPU-based proving framework, whereas ZKTorch serves for high-performance CPU-intensive environments. Framework zkCNN (Liu et al . , 2021 ) Mystique (Weng et al . , 2021 ) pvCNN (Weng et al . , 2022 ) ZKTorch ResNet-50 304 KB* 1.27 GB 343 MB* 85.41 KB 6.4. Ablation Studies To understand the power of the parallel accumulation scheme in ZKTorch , we compare the runtimes of the prover and verifier between ZKTorch and the sequential accumulation based on Mira (Miranda, 2021 ) across five representative models, as shown in Table 7 . ZkTorch consistently outperforms Mira in both proving and verification phases, demonstrating significant runtime improvements. For example, in the case of GPT-j, the proving time is reduced from 8663s to 1398s, a 6.2 × 6.2\times 6.2 × speedup, while the verifier time drops from 239s to 63s. BERT sees a 1.3 × 1.3\times 1.3 × speedup in both proving and verification. The most substantial gain is observed in ResNet-50, where proving time is halved and verification time improves by over 16 × 16\times 16 × . Similar trends are observed for LLaMA-2 and RNNT. These results highlight the optimization of accumulation protocol introduced in ZKTorch , which reduces end-to-end overhead while preserving correctness guarantees and the same size of the resulting proof. To evaluate the effectiveness of the ZKTorch compiler, we compare the proving time, verification time, and proof size before and after compilation across three representative models: GPT-j, BERT, and ResNet-50. As shown in Table 6 , ZkTorch yields substantial reductions across all metrics. For GPT-j, proving time is reduced from 5181s to 1398s (a 3.7 × 3.7\times 3.7 × speedup), while verification time drops from 383s to 63s. Similarly, BERT demonstrates a dramatic improvement, with proving time decreasing from 52977s to 880s. Notably, proof size is also significantly compressed from 155 MB to just 4.88 MB. ResNet-50 shows consistent improvements, with a 4.8 × 4.8\times 4.8 × reduction in proving time and a 2 , 398 × 2,398\times 2 , 398 × reduction in proof size. These results highlight the compiler’s effectiveness in optimizing zk-SNARK execution pipelines, reducing both computational and communication overhead while maintaining functional correctness. Related Work ZK-SNARK protocols. ZK-SNARKs have been widely studied in the security community. These protocols include protocols with different properties. For example, Groth16 has constant verification time with a per-circuit setup (Groth, 2016 ) , while Spartan has linear proving time but longer proofs (Setty, 2019 ) . These protocols have also been specialized for other applications, such as general-purpose ZK virtual machines (Succinct, 2025 ) and ML, which we discuss below. Our work utilizes KZG vector commitments (Kate et al . , 2010 ) and draws inspiration from prior protocols, including Aurora (Ben-Sasson et al . , 2018 ) . ZK-SNARKs for ML. Researchers have developed ZK-SNARK protocols to perform ML inference. These protocols broadly fall under two categories. The first category are compilers that take the computation in ML inference and compile them to generic ZK-SNARK protocols. These include ezkl, zkml, and others (Zkonduit Inc., 2023 ; Chen et al . , 2024 ) . These compilers compile layers into a circuit in the halo2 framwork, a general ZK-SNARK framework, which is based on Plonk, a general ZK-SNARK proving system. Because generic ZK-SNARK protocols are universal, these systems can support a wide range of ML models. However, they suffer in performance compared to specialized systems. Instead, the basic blocks in ZKTorch contain custom protocols for different common operations and ZKTorch compiles layers into a graph of basic blocks. The second category are specialized protocols for specific kinds of models. These include specialized protocols for CNNs (Liu et al . , 2021 ) and LLMs (Sun et al . , 2024 ) . While they are more efficient than generic ZK-SNARK protocols, they do not support a wider range of models. Furthermore, ZKTorch is the first general ZKML framework that supports accumulation scheme to our best knowledge. This allows ZKTorch to produce proofs that are even much smaller than specialized protocols such as zkCNN. In this work, we develop a specialized compiler that generates custom protocols for AI and ML models, bridging the gap between generic ZK-SNARK protocols and specialized protocols for specific model types. Accumulation for ZK-SNARKs. In ZKTorch , we use accumulation to make the proof size of repeated basic blocks constant size. We adapt Mira (Beal and Fisch, 2024 ) , a accumulation scheme designed for pairing-based arguments. Mira verification is inherently sequential and the work only discusses how to support few protocols (e.g., CQ and CQLin) with the scheme. We extend Mira for improved parallelism, and also apply this scheme to 10 more basic blocks. 8. Conclusion We present ZKTorch , a general-purpose framework for compiling machine learning models into zero-knowledge proofs. ZKTorch decomposes high-level models into a directed acyclic graph of basic cryptographic blocks, enabling support for a wide range of architectures including CNNs, RNNs, and transformers. In total, ZKTorch supports 61 commonly used ML layers. To improve scalability, ZKTorch extends the Mira accumulation scheme with a parallelized aggregation protocol, allowing efficient composition of many proof instances and significantly reducing overall proving time. This parallel accumulation mechanism plays a central role in making proof generation feasible for large-scale models. ZKTorch further incorporates a compiler that optimizes the proof graph by applying structure-aware transformations. Finally, our experiments across diverse ML models shows the benefits ZKTorch achieves with respect to speed, accuracy, and generalizability. (2) Knowledge soundness We argue knowledge soundness of the poly-IOP in AGM. The proof sketch is as follows.
